| **[IAMRAGBENCH: Benchmarking Authorization Expansions in LLM-Generated AWS IAM Policies under RAG Poisoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03431v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) using retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can synthesize Amazon Web Services (AWS) Identity and Access Management (IAM) policies from tasks and operational documents. Attacker-controlled context may induce provider-valid policies that exceed trusted authorization requirements. We present IAMRAGBENCH, a 60-case benchmark spanning scope, conditions, explicit denies, trust, permissions boundaries, and role delegation. It combines deterministic authorization-envelope scoring with case-specific validation, including request-level checks where supported. An attack requires a provider-valid clean policy that satisfies the task and a provider-valid treatment policy that violates the envelope, matches a predeclared goal, preserves an intended request, and newly authorizes a forbidden request. Across five endpoints, included-context exposure produces 49 exact authorization deltas, including 46 goal-matched attacks (46/292; 15.75\%). End-to-end BM25 over 720 documents retrieves the designated poison for all 60 tasks at \(k=5\) but produces 7/294 goal-matched attacks (2.38\%). Presenting the same weakening as a final security architecture exception produces 74/116 attacks in a frozen two-endpoint stress test (63.79\%) and 115/294 in a post hoc all-five extension using the same frozen prompts (39.12\%). In a replay of 194 provider-valid treatment policies that violate task requirements, AWS \texttt{CheckNoNewAccess} reports no new access for four of 168 goal-matched exact attacks. The full pre-deployment rule rejects all 171 exact deltas and routes five unresolved cases to review. These findings support treating IAM copilots as untrusted proposal generators whose outputs require independent authorization checks before deployment.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, 4 figures, 5 tables</p></details> |
| **[IdeaTaste: Do LLMs Share the Research Taste of Human Researchers?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03424v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Research taste, the judgment of what to read, which problems to pursue, and which methods to try, increasingly shapes the work of AI scientists, yet it remains far less understood than their ability to execute research. We introduce IdeaTaste, a benchmark that scores an LLM by how closely the research proposals it writes from a literature snapshot frozen at 2025 match the work the same communities published in 2026. Our experiments span six contemporary language models and four research areas from established to emerging fields, covering more than 90{,}000 proposals in total. LLM agents fall well short of human communities in both the literature they cite and the ideas they state, reaching about 26\% of the human level on citation matching and only 18\% on idea matching. Their populations can cite as diversely as a community and take up its tasks and methods, yet rarely arrive at the concrete approaches and research plans it pursues. Neither richer field context nor larger populations substantially close this gap. In blinded expert review, AI proposals trail the 2026 papers least in presentation and most in expected success, echoing their gap in concrete approaches, and those whose ideas no human paper shares fare worst, suggesting weaker directions rather than overlooked opportunities. These results indicate that the main bottleneck in AI research taste lies less in finding the directions a community works on than in turning them into the concrete approaches it goes on to pursue. We release IdeaTaste as an open, continuously updated leaderboard (https://cxcscmu.github.io/IdeaTaste) that benchmarks contemporary AI proposals against future publication cycles.</p></details> |  |
| **[Probing the Limits of the Lie Detector Approach to LLM Deception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.10003v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A strand of mechanistic approaches to deception in large language models (LLMs) relies on "lie detectors" - truth probes aimed to identify model outputs that are internally represented as false. This lie detector approach to LLM deception implicitly assumes that deception is coextensive with lying. This paper challenges that assumption by experimentally investigating whether LLMs can produce deceptive statements that are not false and whether truth probes (if naively applied as deception detectors) fail to detect such behavior. In doing so, we make four core contributions: First, we provide a dataset of manually written examples of deceptive non-falsities grounded in linguistic theory. Second, we show across six open-source LLMs of different sizes and families that some models can select these deceptive non-falsities in a forced-choice task. Third, we demonstrate that while truth probes trained on a standard true-false dataset perform well at detecting false outputs, they perform significantly worse at detecting deceptive non-falsities. We view this as substantiating the worry that naive applications of truth probes as deception detectors suffer from a critical blind spot. Fourth, we explore whether training deception probes in dialogical contexts or including misleading non-false statements in the training improves the deception detection rate.</p></details> | <details><summary>The d...</summary><p>The dataset has been reworked and validated, experiments have been reconducted, and a broader selection of models than in the first version has been investigated</p></details> |
| **[MAS-Guard: Unified Guardrails from Individual Agents to Multi-Agent LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28397v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic AI extends large language models (LLMs) from generating responses to planning and executing complex workflows, substantially expanding their utility while exposing applications to unsafe actions, information leakage, and adversarial manipulation. Multi-agent systems further expand these capabilities through specialization and collaboration, but distributed authority, heterogeneous roles, and opaque or potentially compromised participants make safeguarding even more challenging. Existing defenses address important individual threats but provide fragmented coverage across local execution, system-level interactions, and enforcement mechanisms. We introduce MAS-Guard, a unified framework that closes this gap for safeguarding single- and multi-agent AI applications containing both instrumentable and opaque agents. Within instrumentable agents, the proposed local Agent Guardrail (A-Guard) layer applies task-specific experts to assess agentic events. At the system level, developer-owned Agent Charters and a Control Plane combine deterministic structural checks of routes, agent capabilities, and message formats with semantic assessments of roles and message content. These complementary layers enable enforcement through a shared set of interventions: allow, reflection, confirmation, and block. Evaluated across six benchmarks covering diverse single- and multi-agent scenarios with three agent LLMs from different providers, MAS-Guard reduces the overall attack success rate by 71.5-86.3% relative to unguarded agents, while meeting the utility reference criterion on benign tasks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Same Outputs, Different Scores: Auditing the Unchecked Graders of Multimodal LLM Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03377v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Every multimodal-LLM multiple-choice benchmark ends with a scoring rule that converts a model's response, often free-form text rather than a clean option letter, into a right/wrong verdict, buried in its code. Unlike the dataset, this rule is chosen without justification and left unexamined, yet it sets the published number. We reproduce the official scorers of eight peer-reviewed benchmarks on five open vision-language models (VLMs) that fit a single 16 GB GPU, and introduce a blinded human-plus-LLM-judge adjudication (Cohen's kappa=0.956) that recovers what the model actually answered. The scoring rule alone moves accuracy by up to 22 points on identical outputs, yet exactly 0 wherever a model emits clean option letters, so it hides on strong-model leaderboards. It is the fallback and not the rambling that does the damage: the benchmark with the highest non-compliance we measure has an artifact of exactly zero, because its scorer abstains instead of guessing; a reasoning model with non-compliance 0.000 still swings 9.5 points with which regex match of its trace the parser keeps. No standard rule is reliable: snapping a non-answer to its closest option credits answers never given yet buries correct ones, abstention discards answers stated in words, and likelihood scoring follows answer-token priors, so a single grade conflates capability with luck rather than measuring either. We release all code, labels, and the protocol, and argue benchmarks should report a non-compliance rate and at least two rules, not one unexamined grade.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at WACV 2027 (Evaluations and Datasets Track). Code and data: https://github.com/ahmadhasan2k8/grader-audit</p></details> |
| **[DARE: Aligning LLM Agents with the R Statistical Ecosystem via Distribution-Aware Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.04743v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Model (LLM) agents excel at automating data science workflows, yet often underutilize rigorous statistical methods in R. Existing Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) approaches rely on semantic similarity, ignoring the data-distribution and structural characteristics crucial for determining a statistical method's applicability. To address this, we propose DARE (Distribution-Aware Retrieval Embedding), a representation learning framework incorporating these data characteristics into R function retrieval. Our contributions are fourfold: (i) RPKB, a curated knowledge base of 8,191 high-quality R functions; (ii) DARE, the distribution-aware retrieval framework; (iii) RCodingAgent, an R-oriented agent integrating DARE for end-to-end analysis; and (iv) RStatBench, a comprehensive benchmark of 100 statistical tasks. Experiments demonstrate that DARE improves NDCG@10 by 13.43 to 32.65 percentage points across evaluated embedding models, with a MiniLM-based DARE achieving 93.92% NDCG@10 alongside high efficiency. Furthermore, integrating DARE into RCodingAgent nearly doubles mean downstream accuracy on RStatBench, increasing from 31.8% to 62.2% across ten LLMs. This work helps bridge modern LLM automation with R's established statistical computing ecosystem.</p></details> | <details><summary>32 pa...</summary><p>32 pages, 8 figures, and 3 tables</p></details> |
| **[The SIMPLE Law for Energy Cost of LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03330v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generative AI is billed per token, yet tokens are not equally expensive. Each token enters the KV-cache and raises the cost of those that follow, so energy grows non-linearly along a sequence whose tokens look identical. No analytical model states what a token costs, and the most accurate SOTA model is a fit, not a law: it absorbs architecture into fitted coefficients, requiring per-LLM fitting. We derive closed-form expressions of the computational and memory-access complexity of LLM inference in terms of architectural hyperparameters, covering MHA and GQA, and compare a family of analytical models of increasing granularity. From these, we obtain the SIMPLE Inference Model to Predict LLMs Energy (SIMPLE), a four-parameter law that separates prefill from decode, and decode into KV-cache cost and next-token computation. Over traces of 13 dense LLMs from six families on NVIDIA H100 GPUs, it attains R2 = 0.969 and 11.97% MAPE, 3.1-3.5x below the SOTA, without per-LLM calibration. The SIMPLE law explains as well as predicts: prefill energy grows quadratically with the hidden dimension, so larger LLMs suffer most from long inputs; and more aggressive GQA KV grouping reduces the per-token energy by up to 3x relative to standard MHA. Increasing the reasoning token budget pushes output length beyond the analytically derived energy minimum, driving super-linear energy growth; although accuracy grows with the budget, energy grows faster, so Intelligence per Joule peaks at an intermediate budget and falls by up to 3.14x across a 4.0x reasoning-budget range. Agentic serving raises energy per request by 13.0x relative to non-agentic ones, driven by the growth of both the prefill and the KV-cache terms with input length; while prefix caching removes most of the prefill cost (-93%), the KV-cache term is unaffected, so even a cached agentic request costs 4.8x more.</p></details> | <details><summary>Submi...</summary><p>Submitted to Future Generation Computer Systems</p></details> |
| **[LLMs Depend on Specialist Tools for Out-of-Distribution Generalization in MS/MS Structural Elucidation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03308v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A frontier language model was recently reported to solve 1D NMR structural elucidation without tools. We ask whether this capability transfers to tandem mass spectrometry (MS/MS) in the de novo setting, where the model must propose a structure directly from the spectrum without a candidate list or reference library. To separate reasoning from recall, we build a 100-analyte benchmark from MassSpecGym, split by each analyte's textual footprint into in-distribution (well documented in literature) and out-of-distribution (essentially undocumented) arms. We hold the model, harness, and output format fixed and vary only the LLM's access to domain-specific tools. Without tools, a frontier LLM reaches 52% Top-1 accuracy in-distribution but only 2% out-of-distribution. Only access to de novo structure generators improves out-of-distribution accuracy, raising it to as much as 38% Top-1, and the resulting agent outperforms the standalone de novo models both in and out of distribution. These results suggest that LLMs are not effective at de novo MS/MS structural elucidation on their own, and that further gains depend on the specialist tools available to the agent rather than on the LLM itself.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at "NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on Agentic Systems for Molecular Sciences" and "I Can't Believe It's Not Better: Failure Modes of AI in Biology Workshop at NeurIPS 2026"</p></details> |
| **[ENGINE: Endogenous Variational MultiScale Optimization for Zeroth-Order LLM Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03293v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Zeroth-order (ZO) optimization enables memory-efficient large language model (LLM) fine-tuning, and recent methods incorporate curvature-aware strategies. However, in the high-dimensional parameter space of LLMs, these methods inherently suffer from exploding gradient variance and prohibitive costs. Conversely, confining updates to a low-dimensional subspace reduces variance but discards critical high-dimensional information, degrading performance. This creates a fundamental dilemma: full-space optimization is informative but has high variance, while subspace optimization has low variance but loses information. To break this trade-off, we propose ENGINE (Endogenous Variational MultiScale). Inspired by computational mechanics, we adapt static Variational MultiScale (VMS) theory to dynamic optimization, treating low- and high-dimensional spaces as resolved coarse and unresolved fine scales. Through a multigrid-style V-cycle, ENGINE alternates between spaces, dynamically probing fine-grained information in the high-dimensional space and injecting it to correct the efficient low-dimensional sampling process. Under appropriate assumptions, statistical analysis characterizes variance reduction and correction of curvature-induced bias, and convergence analysis establishes a stationarity bound for the corrected coarse updates. Evaluations on standard LLM benchmarks show that ENGINE achieves higher accuracy and faster convergence than existing ZO methods, achieving up to a $2.61\times$ speed-up in forward passes with zero additional peak memory overhead. Code is available at https://github.com/AI4Science-WestlakeU/ENGINE.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026, 32 pages, 9 figures</p></details> |
| **[MBBQ: A Dataset for Cross-Lingual Comparison of Stereotypes in Generative LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.07243v5)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generative large language models (LLMs) have been shown to exhibit harmful biases and stereotypes. While safety fine-tuning typically takes place in English, if at all, these models are being used by speakers of many different languages. There is existing evidence that the performance of these models is inconsistent across languages and that they discriminate based on demographic factors of the user. Motivated by this, we investigate whether the social stereotypes exhibited by LLMs differ as a function of the language used to prompt them, while controlling for cultural differences and task accuracy. To this end, we present MBBQ (Multilingual Bias Benchmark for Question-answering), a carefully curated version of the English BBQ dataset extended to Dutch, Spanish, and Turkish, which measures stereotypes commonly held across these languages. We further complement MBBQ with a parallel control dataset to measure task performance on the question-answering task independently of bias. Our results based on several open-source and proprietary LLMs confirm that some non-English languages suffer from bias more than English, even when controlling for cultural shifts. Moreover, we observe significant cross-lingual differences in bias behaviour for all except the most accurate models. With the release of MBBQ, we hope to encourage further research on bias in multilingual settings. The dataset and code are available at https://github.com/Veranep/MBBQ.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to COLM 2024</p></details> |
| **[R$^2$-Omni: Reducing Structural and Precision Redundancy in Omni-LLMs for Long-Form Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03247v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Omni-modal large language models (Omni-LLMs) typically process large numbers of multimodal tokens from audio-visual inputs, incurring substantial computational costs and a large memory footprint for the key-value (KV) cache. Aggressive token pruning can alleviate this problem while maintaining competitive performance on multiple-choice tasks, which require only a single option as output. However, we observe substantial quality degradation when generating long-form, open-ended responses. We attribute this gap to query-guided selection, which can retain sufficient evidence for answer selection while discarding details needed for comprehensive generation. This motivates reducing overlapping information across tokens, termed structural redundancy, while preserving diverse content. However, the retained representations may still contain precision redundancy due to unnecessarily high numerical precision in their cached values. To address these two levels of redundancy in token representations, we introduce a training-free framework named R$^2$-Omni. First, Structure-Aware Token Consolidation (STC) consolidates redundant representations using spatiotemporal similarity in video and local continuity in audio. Then, Semantic-Sensitivity Guided Precision Allocation (SSPA) allocates value precision to the retained representations according to the estimated reduction in attention-output error under quantized keys, with query attention and audio-visual alignment providing a semantic prior. Extensive experiments on long-form generation benchmarks demonstrate an average 5.0$\times$ reduction in the memory footprint while preserving 97.98% of baseline generation quality and achieving a 1.48$\times$ end-to-end speedup, highlighting the benefits of jointly reducing structural and precision redundancy.</p></details> | 32 pages, 9 figures |
| **[Cold-Start Pricing of LLM Services under Two-Sided Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03199v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Online large language model (LLM) services face a pricing cold start: providers initially lack the historical data and user information needed for precise pricing, while users' willingness to pay evolves with repeated use. We formulate this challenge as a \emph{two-sided learning} problem between a provider and a user. The user learns from private usage experience, whereas the provider incurs a serving cost and dynamically adapts prices using only purchase decisions to maximize cumulative profit. The provider's central challenge is to learn to price against an evolving willingness to pay without observing the private experiences driving that evolution. We establish matching upper and lower bounds on the minimax regret over horizon $T$, identifying two cost-dependent regimes. When serving costs are sufficiently low, our Calibration-and-Tracking (CAT) algorithm achieves the optimal rate $Θ(T^{1/3})$. In the higher-cost regime, where the provider must learn whether continued service is profitable, Calibration-and-Tracking with Retirement (CAT-R) attains the optimal rate $Θ(\sqrt{T})$. This phase transition shows that serving costs affect not only profit margins but also the fundamental difficulty of learning to price LLM services. Simulations support the predicted regret scaling and show that our algorithms outperform standard online learning baselines in the evaluated settings.</p></details> |  |
| **[Finding the Pivots to Repair Broken Source-Test Evolution Using LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03197v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Repairing obsolete tests in response to source code evolution is critical for test suite reliability and software quality assurance. While large language models (LLMs) have emerged as promising tools for automating many software development tasks, the effective supply of context and the orchestration of repair pipelines in this source--test co-evolution scenario remain poorly understood. Moreover, although reasoning is found helpful for LLMs on some complex coding tasks, it remains unclear which reasoning steps actually contribute to effective test repairs and how the insights learned from LLMs' behaviors benefit coding agents. In this paper, we present a systematic empirical study of test repair for source-test co-evolution empowered by LLMs, investigating the effective setups and typical LLM behaviors. To support this study, we construct a new benchmark of 500 real-world source-test co-evolution cases from commits submitted between May 2024 and April 2026 across 15 highly-starred open-source Java projects. Our experimental results reveal several key insights for effective LLM-based test repair: the complementary benefits of combining test failure symptoms, source code changes, and supportive code context, as well as the effectiveness of self-repair rounds. We further derive a common reasoning action taxonomy from LLMs' reasoning traces, which proves instrumental in guiding LLMs to repair tests more effectively while at a lower financial cost. Applying our findings to the Codex agent further improves its repair success rate on challenging cases by 66.7%.</p></details> |  |
| **[Verification Allocation in Stylized LLM Agent Pipelines under Hidden Tool Degradation: A Discrete-Time Mean-Field Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03192v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We study a stylized verification-allocation model for role-structured pipelines with binary inherited corruption and hidden tool degradation. Standard contraction arguments give serial and multi-role fixed points and a finite-population deviation bound under independence. When verification actions affect neither future errors nor observations, the belief-state choice is myopic. We characterize a two-action threshold under an explicit monotonicity condition and a restricted four-action interval and tail policy when generation rates are equal and only one check degrades. Synthetic experiments check these model-specific results and find counterexamples outside the restriction. This is conditional model analysis, not a general mean-field-game result or empirical evidence about deployed LLM systems.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 3 figures. Code and results: https://github.com/Shereen20112003/verification-allocation-hidden-tool-degradation</p></details> |
| **[Controlling Sharpening in LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03146v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM post-training via reinforcement learning has shown consistent improvements in accuracy at the cost of reducing behavioral coverage - a phenomenon known as sharpening that can stall progress on difficult tasks. Repeated sampling objectives such as $\mathrm{pass@k}$ and $\mathrm{max@k}$ are known to mitigate sharpening, but the mechanism behind it remains poorly understood. Moreover, existing approaches for $\mathrm{@k}$ optimization use dedicated algorithms, and no general framework exists for adapting established policy optimization methods from $\mathrm{@}1$ to $\mathrm{@k}$. We derive the optimal policies for KL-regularized $\mathrm{pass@k}$ and $\mathrm{max@k}$ objectives in closed form. Our explicit solutions reveal that $\mathrm{@k}$ optimization naturally redistributes learning pressure based on prompt difficulty, directing it toward harder problems. Crucially, we show that standard $\mathrm{@}1$ objectives achieve these exact same optimal policies through prompt-adaptive regularization or reward transformation. Ultimately, this yields an optimizer-independent explanation for why $\mathrm{@k}$ objectives reduce sharpening on easy tasks while prioritizing success on harder ones. Building on these insights, we introduce $\mathrm{@k}$ Adaptive Pressure for Policy Optimization (KAPPO). Following a one-time reference calibration, KAPPO seamlessly adapts existing $\mathrm{@}1$ methods to $\mathrm{@k}$ via prompt-adaptive regularization or reward transformation, without otherwise altering their training procedures. Experiments with several RL methods on mathematical reasoning and code generation demonstrate that KAPPO provides direct control over sharpening. Crucially, we demonstrate that $\mathrm{@k}$ pressure can be fully realized with single completion training, matching the performance of multi-completion $\mathrm{@k}$ optimizers at a fraction of the cost.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Local Skills to Long-Horizon Web Tasks: Progressive Skill Exploration for LLM Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03116v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are driving autonomous agents toward real-world environments, with the Web emerging as one of the most representative interaction scenarios. However, training Web agents with naive exploration remains challenging: successful trajectories are rare, and the sparse terminal rewards make it difficult for agents to obtain useful feedback from failed trajectories. Inspired by skill acquisition theory, we propose Skill-Composed Progressive Exploration (SCOPE), a short-to-long learning framework for long-horizon Web agents that decomposes complex task exploration into a gradual expansion process from local skills to complete task-solving capability. Specifically, SCOPE iteratively explores around local skills: Skill Discovery extracts reusable local skills and failure experiences from historical rollouts; Skill Orchestration composes discovered skills into high-level skill chains; and Skill Expansion leverages failure experiences aligned with the current skill chain to guide the agent toward exploring new subskills. Through this process, SCOPE progressively constructs task-completing skill chains across rollouts and internalizes them into the policy parameters through agentic on-policy distillation. Extensive experiments on WebArena, WebVoyager, and WebShop show that SCOPE consistently improves exploration dynamics and achieves 3.9% to 18.6% relative gains over the strongest baselines in each setting.</p></details> | <details><summary>31 pa...</summary><p>31 pages, 9 figures. Accepted at NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Preprocessing Choices in Fine-Tuning Data Matter: Utility and Demographic Sensitivity in LLM-Based Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03113v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) have introduced a generative approach to recommendation, producing item lists from textual representations of user histories and item information. However, this generative setting raises challenges concerning output validity, prompt consistency, and sensitivity to demographic descriptors. In this work, we investigate how preprocessing choices in fine-tuning data affect recommendation utility and fairness-related behavior in a generative LLM-based recommender system. We first establish a controlled recommendation pipeline that combines supervised fine-tuning, catalog-constrained decoding, and prompt augmentation. We then study three preprocessing choices: item-title representation, demographic balancing, and auxiliary item-knowledge enrichment. Experiments on MovieLens-1M show that the catalog-constrained pipeline eliminates out-of-catalog items and ensures complete ten-item outputs, while prompt augmentation improves prompt consistency and utility. Original MovieLens titles increase HR@10 from 10.67% to 12.47% and Coverage@10 from 41.36% to 47.88%. Fairness-related effects are attribute-dependent: age balancing and knowledge enrichment reduce selected group-level utility gaps while largely preserving utility, whereas gender balancing reduces utility without improving the evaluated gender-related indicators. Overall, preprocessing choices in fine-tuning data should be evaluated jointly in terms of utility, output behavior, and demographic sensitivity.</p></details> | <details><summary>11 pa...</summary><p>11 pages, 2 figures, 5 tables. Accepted at the 3rd Workshop on Evaluating and Applying Recommender Systems with Large Language Models (EARL) at CIKM 2026, November 8, 2026, Rome, Italy</p></details> |
| **[COVE: A Task-Adaptive, Code-Based Ensemble for Proactive Verification of LLM Outputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03111v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) are now embedded in enterprise workflows, yet their outputs remain prone to hallucinations: plausible-sounding but factually incorrect or malformed content. Most verification approaches are reactive, detecting errors only after generation and often spending additional expensive LLM calls to do so. We present COVE, a task-adaptive, code-based ensemble that takes the opposite stance: it prevents errors before generation and validates structure and consistency afterwards using six deterministic, pure-Python innovations that add no extra LLM calls. On an internal evaluation spanning 11 text datasets (1,020 examples) and 4 image datasets (200 examples), COVE lifts text accuracy from a 45.2% unverified baseline to 82.1% (an 81.7% relative improvement), improves image accuracy by 69.5%, and reduces token usage by 48 to 96% (simulated cost model), at a measured verification overhead of 0.26 ms per output (excluding the LLM call). This article describes the engineering design of each innovation (with pseudocode and the exact parameters we use), the measured impact, an ablation of every component, and the practical lessons learned from deploying code-based verification in enterprise pipelines.</p></details> | <details><summary>6 pag...</summary><p>6 pages. An earlier version is available on Zenodo: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23636512</p></details> |
| **[An Experimental Study of Hybrid Retrieval, NLI and Open-source LLMs for Scientific Claim Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03093v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Claim verification is the task of deciding whether a source text supports a claim, contradicts it, or lacks sufficient evidence. Natural Language Inference (NLI) models can judge whether a single sentence supports or contradicts a claim, but they struggle when the evidence is spread across several sentences. Conversely, Large Language Models (LLMs) can reason across sentences, but may draw conclusions the text does not support. In this experimental study, we test whether a conservative fusion rule, labeling a claim Support or Contradict only when both models agree and defaulting to Not Enough Evidence otherwise, combines their strengths. We construct a benchmark of 2,035 claims from SciFact and CliniFact (858 Support, 152 Contradict, 1,025 Not Enough Evidence). We retrieve the top five most relevant sentences per claim using a hybrid of semantic and lexical (BM25) retrieval, which slightly outperforms either method alone. These retrieved sentences are then provided to every verification model. We then compare a DeBERTa-v3-base-mnli-fever-anli NLI baseline against Llama 3.1 8B, Qwen3 8B, and Mistral 7B. Qwen3 achieves the highest Macro-F1 (0.7937), followed by Llama 3.1 (0.7204) and Mistral (0.6388), while the NLI baseline reaches only 0.3157. Contrary to our hypothesis, fusion degrades performance, dropping Macro-F1 to 0.7392, 0.6730, and 0.5991, respectively. To understand this decline, we analyze agreements, disagreements, and common error patterns between the NLI and LLM verifiers. Our error analysis shows that NLI and LLM disagreements often result in correct LLM predictions being overturned.</p></details> | 15 pages |
| **[WebWeaver: Breaking Topology Confidentiality in LLM Multi-Agent Systems with Stealthy Context-Based Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.11132v3)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Communication topology is a critical factor in the utility and safety of LLM-based multi-agent systems (LLM-MAS), making it a high-value intellectual property (IP) whose confidentiality remains insufficiently studied. Existing topology inference attempts rely on impractical assumptions, including control over the administrative agent and direct identity queries via jailbreaks, which are easily defeated by basic keyword-based defenses. As a result, prior analyses fail to capture the real-world threat of such attacks. To bridge this realism gap, we propose \textit{WebWeaver}, an attack framework that infers the complete LLM-MAS topology by compromising only a single arbitrary agent instead of the administrative agent. Unlike prior approaches, WebWeaver relies solely on agent contexts rather than agent IDs, enabling significantly stealthier inference. WebWeaver further introduces a new covert jailbreak-based mechanism and a novel fully jailbreak-free diffusion design to handle cases where jailbreaks fail. Additionally, we address a key challenge in diffusion-based inference by proposing a masking strategy that preserves known topology during diffusion, with theoretical guarantees of correctness. Extensive experiments show that WebWeaver substantially outperforms state-of-the-art (SOTA) baselines, achieving about 60\% higher inference accuracy under active defenses with negligible overhead.</p></details> |  |

| **[Does the Judge See What the Evaluation Requires? Input Assurance for LLM Agent Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02676v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>An LLM judge assesses agent steps from text packets. In one pipeline with 30 trajectories, matching hashes, passing checkers and successful staging (copying packets for the judge) coexisted with violations of all four obligations, the last two in integration tests. The renderer omitted every structured tool call, leaving 53 of 60 judged steps blank despite matching hashes. For one judge configuration, later context raised error flags by 13 more on error than on correct steps. Restoring every call reduced this contrast to 2, a reduction of 11 (0.37 per trajectory, 95% cluster-bootstrap interval 0.17 to 0.53). In packets ending at the judged step, that configuration abstained at least once on every blank step, while the other four sometimes abstained but also flagged blank error and correct steps. Both separately written checkers accepted an unmatched tool result. In integration tests, a stub replacing the judge read a failed build's packet and a copy changed after staging. Three added controls stopped 18 of 19 constructed fault cases before the stub read any packet. The remaining stale script never ran. | **[When Does Multi-Agent RL Improve LLM Workflows? Under SP, per-output token averaging sets each role's gradient share, a hidden hyperparameter absent from single-agent RL, and the solving roles learn slowly; reweighting it gives SP the speed and peak of IP, and its decline. Under IP, a parallel role, whose instances share one adapter, takes one optimizer step per instance per training step and can drift after the peak as its entropy rises and accuracy declines. However, fixed thresholds break the budget on new streams, and per-entry rules need labels on every entry before reusing it. We find an unspent error budget, as vCache realizes only 12-55% of $δ$ on three public query streams. Surplus spends this budget where reuse is most likely correct. Free labels from every LLM call and a ledger of realized errors keep this threshold safe under drift. On five streams, including ORCAS search queries, Surplus holds $δ$ in every test cell, which no baseline does. | **[RECEIPT: Measuring and Repairing the Return Assumption of LLM Agents in Web Service Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01930v1)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents operate Web infrastructure from the command line and rely on a return assumption, namely that each tool call returns within a bounded time and with its requested action finished. Many commands, however, ask a question before they act, and the agent's harness either closes the program's input or keeps it open as a terminal at which nobody types. With closed input, the program can return status 0 without the requested action. With open input, it waits until a time limit, and the agent pays in time and tokens. To the best of our knowledge, this paper is the first to measure and repair the return assumption. We collect 91,469 production tool calls and 89,047 public agent trajectories, test how 8 widely used harnesses launch commands, and build Litmus, a catalog of 158 checked command-line invocations, and Tripwire, a benchmark of 25 task pairs that differ only in whether a question guards the requested action. We then propose RECEIPT, which keeps the input open, reads what each process waits for from system calls, and returns this state to an unmodified agent through a model-checked protocol. On Litmus, closed input causes 11.5% of tested invocations to return status 0 without performing the requested action, and 7 of 8 harnesses close input when no terminal is attached. An unanswered question at a plain terminal multiplies a task's token use by 2.9, and the input connection moves an LLM from 7th to 3rd place among 8. Across 5 LLMs, RECEIPT reduces false claims of success by 73.5% and completes all 25 guarded tasks on a second harness. An agent's reliability therefore depends on what its harness lets it observe as well as on its LLM. Agent builders should report what each tool call did, not only how it ended, and benchmark maintainers should fix and report the input connection before they rank LLMs.</p></details> |  |
| **[Does reflective LLM feedback survive a wet-lab-scale label budget?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01908v1)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Can reflective language-model feedback improve a biological predictor when each cycle buys only one plate of labels? We separate three possible sources of benefit: proposing model edits, choosing measurements, and revising decisions through repeated feedback. We replay $36$ ProteinGym assays, starting with $96$ labels and acquiring $96$ per round for six rounds, with a sealed test set and a shared edit schema. The pre-specified comparison of full LLM control against random edits detects no advantage in test rank correlation ($Δz=-0.008$, $95\%$ CI $[-0.034,+0.018]$); equivalence is also unestablished. Exploratory ablations nevertheless identify useful model editing. With random acquisition fixed, LLM edits improve Fisher-transformed test Spearman by $0.043$ over no edits; with acquisition fixed to upper confidence bound, they improve normalized top-variant recovery by $0.023$. LLM acquisition alone does not detectably outperform fixed adaptive rules, and repeated feedback does not detectably outperform one initial recommendation under full control. Thus, the observed gains support constrained specialist editing, while the incremental value of recursive reflection remains unestablished at this label budget.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the NeurIPS 2026 Agentic AI for Biological Discovery Workshop. OpenReview: https://openreview.net/forum?id=ezGV5BIXVJ</p></details> |
| **[Don't Guess, Ask Wisely: Benchmarking and Training Proactive Elicitation in Open-Domain LLM Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01878v1)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When user queries are incomplete, large language models should ask targeted questions before committing to an answer, a capability we call proactive elicitation, yet evaluation on real user-assistant queries across seven LLMs (8B to frontier) shows that fewer than 11% of underspecified queries trigger a follow-up question. To benchmark and train this capability, we introduce VagueBench, where each seed query is annotated with structured intent and detail units then degraded into intent-vague and fully-vague variants via controlled removal of linguistic cues, yielding known hidden information sets for evaluating both when and what to ask. We propose DialogGRPO, an offline reinforcement learning method that organizes multi-turn elicitation as a dialogue-state directed acyclic graph, where candidate questions at each node receive dense turn-level rewards combining immediate information gain with lookahead value through group-relative comparison, enabling fine-grained credit assignment without runtime simulation. DialogGRPO elicits missing information on over 88% of vague queries with substantial gains in both recovery efficiency and answer coverage, without over-asking on complete queries, outperforms prompting and test-time-scaling baselines, and generalizes zero-shot to MultiWOZ 2.4 and AmbigQA.</p></details> | <details><summary>26 pa...</summary><p>26 pages, 6 figures; EMNLP 2026 oral</p></details> |
| **[NEIGHBOR: Aligning Service Behaviour and Evidence Use in an LLM Agent for Community Resident Services](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01836v1)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Residents who contact a community service desk about a repair or a service charge need answers they can act on, with facts that hold for their case and commitments the desk can keep. In tool-augmented LLM agents, such facts come from documents and records, and the conduct of a service answer is usually specified in a system prompt. We present NEIGHBOR, an LLM agent for community resident services that trains this conduct into the model that drives its tools. NEIGHBOR is deployed on a live resident-service platform for a district of an East Asian city of more than ten million people. It adapts Qwen3-235B-A22B with LoRA on 7,824 supervised fine-tuning examples built from public community-service materials around six service-behaviour targets, among them commitment control and human referral, and the adapted model plans document retrieval and read-only text-to-SQL over the resident's own records. In an offline evaluation with seven configurations, an independent domain expert rated all 350 answers to 50 stratified held-out queries. NEIGHBOR scores 7.74/10 against 5.86 for the same agent running the un-tuned model (+1.88; paired Wilcoxon $p<10^{-8}$; higher on 46 of 50 queries) and improves all six rated dimensions. No tool-augmented answer received a red-line flag (standalone models: 8-14% of answers). An LLM judge ranks the seven configurations much as the expert does (Spearman $ρ=0.93$). Its rubric counts specifics absent from the reference answer as fabricated, so it scores tool-grounded answers 1-2 points lower, enough to change which configuration ranks first. For resident-facing agents, service behaviour is a practical fine-tuning target that raises expert-rated answer quality with the tools held fixed.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 6 figures, 5 tables</p></details> |
| **[When Should LLMs Be Less Specific? Selective Abstraction for Reliable Long-Form Text Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11908v4)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLMs are widely used, yet they remain prone to factual errors that erode trust and limit adoption in high-risk settings. One approach to mitigate this risk is to equip models with uncertainty estimation mechanisms that abstain when confidence is low. However, this binary "all-or-nothing" approach is excessively restrictive in long-form settings, often discarding valuable information. We introduce Selective Abstraction (SA), a framework that enables LLMs to trade specificity for reliability by selectively reducing the detail of uncertain content. We formalize SA through selective risk and coverage, and propose Atom-wise Selective Abstraction, a claim-level instantiation that decomposes responses into atomic claims and replaces uncertain atoms with higher-confidence, less specific abstractions. We develop a novel end-to-end pipeline for evaluating long-form generation that instantiates risk as factual correctness and measures coverage as retained information. Our comprehensive empirical evaluation across five LLMs and two long-form factual benchmarks shows that atom-wise SA consistently improves the risk-coverage trade-off, outperforming seven baselines, including claim removal, self-revision, sampling-based aggregation, and chain-of-verification. It improves AURC by up to 35.61% over claim removal, demonstrating that reducing specificity boosts reliability while preserving substantial information. We further provide a risk-guided procedure for selecting a confidence threshold that satisfies a target risk.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted as poster to NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[SkillDAG: Self-Evolving Typed Skill Graphs for LLM Skill Selection at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03056v4)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large skill libraries are now standard equipment for LLM agents, and which subset reaches the prompt decides what the agent can do. Enumeration and top-$K$ embedding retrieval both treat skills as independent documents, assuming relevance is a property of one skill at a time. It is not: skills depend on, conflict with, specialize, and duplicate one another, so the closest match to a task can name it while omitting a prerequisite it needs. Graph retrieval encodes these relations but spends them in a fixed pipeline, handing the agent an opaque bundle. We present SkillDAG, which exposes a typed skill graph to the LLM as an agent-callable interface rather than consuming it for the agent: search returns vector matches, typed-edge neighbors, and conflict signals, and a propose-then-commit protocol lets it register execution-backed edges as it works. Across two benchmarks and three model families, SkillDAG outperforms full-library prompting, vector retrieval, and Graph-of-Skills, gaining +14.3 points of ALFWorld success and tying only at ceiling; its ranking holds flat across a $10\times$ pool expansion that degrades the fixed pipeline. Inter-skill structure, these results suggest, is worth more to an agent that can query and revise it than to a retriever that consumes it.</p></details> | <details><summary>In *T...</summary><p>In *The 2026 Conference on Empirical Methods in Natural Language Processing (EMNLP, Findings)*, 2026</p></details> |
| **[OmniCast: Interaction-Aware Serving for Real-Time Omni-Modal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22983v2)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Realtime omni-modal LMs support speech-centric conversations where users stream inputs, hear generated audio, and interrupt freely. Existing Omni-LM serving systems still rely on throughput-oriented LLM scheduling and LRU KV offloading. These policies ignore audio playback and multi-turn reuse: they may generate tokens far beyond what users hear, wasting work after barge-in, and evict KV state needed in the next turn. OmniCast is an interaction-aware serving system for realtime Omni-LM interaction. It exposes playback progress, speech activity, and barge-in events to the serving pipeline. The scheduler prioritizes first-audio and near-underrun sessions while limiting generation beyond the playback frontier. The KV manager uses next-use-aware eviction and preloads likely-needed KV during user speech to hide reload latency. On vLLM-Omni, OmniCast improves realtime serving across two Omni-LMs and mixed workloads. It lowers P90 audio TTFP by $1.55\times$ on average and up to $2.21\times$, while improving completed-request throughput by $1.15\times$ on average and up to $1.56\times$, and moves most KV reload work off the next-turn critical path.</p></details> |  |

| **[WebHarbor: Docking Real Websites for Evolving GUI Agent Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03634v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>GUI web agents require realistic and interactive environments that remain stable during both evaluation and training. Live website evaluation suffers from changing content, access restrictions, and limited coverage of authenticated workflows, while existing synthetic environments for training often lack functional depth and visual fidelity. We propose WebHarbor, a suite of 153 executable and dockerized website environments that faithfully reconstruct the visual interfaces, content, and functional workflows of real-world web services under deterministic control. We build WebHarbor through task-driven human-agent collaboration, where environments evolve as new tasks expand their functional scope, coding agents implement the required changes, and human contributors audit visual fidelity, functional correctness, and task validity. The resulting suite comprises 3,518 tasks spanning multi-step and post-authentication workflows, where existing works often fail to cover. Beyond reliable evaluation, WebHarbor provides high-fidelity synthetic web environments for cost-efficient reinforcement learning (RL). We demonstrate that a Qwen 4B-level GUI agent trained with RL in WebHarbor generalizes to unseen tasks and performs competitively with leading open-source GUI web agents across four benchmarks. The code and data are publicly available at https://github.com/aiming-lab/WebHarbor.</p></details> | 31 pages |
| **[Drafting Agents, Not Just Tokens: Speculative Recursive Self-Improvement for Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16491v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Improving an agent through successive revisions requires balancing exploration and evaluation under a limited budget. A narrow search can miss useful sequences of edits, while fully evaluating every intermediate version often exhausts the budget before those sequences yield a verified improvement. Inspired by the draft--verify principle of speculative decoding, we propose Speculative Recursive Self-Improvement (SpecRSI), a two-level framework coupling inner-loop agent search with outer-loop improver learning. SpecRSI drafts successors before their predecessors are fully evaluated, while adopting only fully evaluated, admissible versions. In the inner loop, Verification-Aware Tree Search (VATS) allocates the budget between further edits and outstanding checks using the predicted verified return of a continuation. In the outer loop, Group Verification Optimization (GVO) updates the improver from completed searches while keeping the real evaluator fixed. We evaluate SpecRSI on scientific optimization (including active queue management (AQM) for network infrastructure), coding, and general agent-system benchmarks. Under matched evaluation-call budgets, SpecRSI improves scientific terminal gain over Janus by 60.0%--96.3% and area under the budget curve by 32.1%--91.6%, while achieving higher means across all 50 scientific backbone--task pairs and outperforming MetaRSI-v1 by 1.30--2.70 percentage points (pp) in system score across twenty backbones. On instance-level comparisons, it further improves PyBaMM terminal gain by 142.6% and SWE-Pro success by 6.0 pp at 7.0% and 9.2% lower token cost, respectively. These results demonstrate the efficacy of the proposed approach.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages; updated experiments, analysis, and figures; code available at https://github.com/SpecRSI-GPR/SpecRSI</p></details> |
| **[Agent Skill Evaluation and Evolution: Frameworks and Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11435v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The growth of agent skills has transformed how agentic systems are built, evaluated, and deployed. As skill libraries continue to scale, rigorous evaluation becomes critical to ensuring their utility, quality, and safety in real-world applications. Consequently, the field is undergoing an emerging paradigm shift from isolated skill creation to automated, evaluation-driven skill evolution. In this survey, we systematically examine the landscape of skill evolution and evaluation beyond foundational skill creation. We categorize evolution into four distinct paradigms, spanning execution feedback, trajectory distillation, compression, and reinforcement learning, showing how each element contributes to improving skill utility and reliability. We also provide an analysis of six skill-centric benchmark categories, identifying structural gaps in benchmark coverage, trade-offs, and metric richness to advance skill research. Finally, we identify open directions for building skill ecosystems that are generalizable, efficient, and verifiably safe. The project URL is https://github.com/Cassie07/AgentSkill_Survey</p></details> |  |
| **[Strategist: Evolving Reasoning with Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03618v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A model is only half of an AI agent system. The other half is the inference strategy: the reasoning procedure that turns model calls into a response. Today that second half is written by hand, and the experience of building one strategy never carries to the next. We introduce Strategist, a meta-agent that evolves inference strategies across tasks. Each strategy is written as a blueprint,executable code that other strategies can reuse, and together the blueprints form a library. Given a task and a small development set, Strategist retrieves related blueprints, writes candidate strategies, tests them on real samples, and revises them from the results. Every candidate then enters the library, so later tasks build on earlier discoveries. On 30 benchmarks, Strategist beats the strongest baseline on 24 and matches its average accuracy (74.7% vs.74.0%) at 39% lower inference cost. Its strategies also transfer to unseen tasks and other models without further search. The part of an AI system that is not improved on its own now does.</p></details> | NeurIPS 2026 |
| **[HierFlow: Hierarchical Coupled Dual-Space Search for Automatic Agentic Workflow Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21609v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Although structured workflows empower Large Language Models (LLMs) to tackle complex problems, automating their creation is severely hindered by a vast combinatorial search space, frequently resulting in inflexible and resource-heavy offline training dependencies. To address this, we conceptualize workflow generation as an intertwined topology-and-execution search paradigm, where the broader topological layer dictates subtask boundaries and lower-level execution outcomes actively reshape the topology itself. Building on this foundation, we introduce HierFlow, a training-free, test-time hierarchical search architecture that automates agentic workflow design by merging feedback-guided topology adjustments with a fast, MCTS-inspired tree search for sub-workflow optimization. HierFlow maximizes efficiency through an intelligent gating module that selectively triggers execution-level searches based on contextual necessity, a mechanism we further support with an in-depth analysis detailing how varying degrees of cross-task coupling impact the effectiveness of hierarchical splitting. Comprehensive testing across question answering, mathematical reasoning, and code generation benchmarks confirms that HierFlow consistently outperforms strong baselines, delivering an optimal balance of high-quality results and computational efficiency without any additional training overhead.</p></details> |  |
| **[DiabAgent: Toward Agentic Physiological Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03601v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As pretraining scales, medical language models have advanced rapidly in clinical question answering. Their ability to achieve effective physiological control through sustained intervention still lacks systematic quantitative evaluation. Type 1 diabetes provides a concrete setting: impaired insulin secretion creates dependence on exogenous insulin, with dosing continually adjusted using information about meals, exercise, illness, and glucose measurements. We introduce DiabAgent, a research suite for agentic physiological control comprising the Agentic Physiological Control (APC) framework, the DiabetSim simulator, and the SweetBench benchmark. APC controls glucose through medical-device tools, using ongoing observations to infer individual states, accumulate personal experience, and choose treatment actions. We build DiabetSim to simulate different patients, events, and device conditions for scalable virtual testing and training. Using DiabetSim, we construct SweetBench by instantiating 32 scenario designs as 212 glucose-control tasks. With Claude Opus 5.5 and a fixed decision interval of one hour, APC achieves a COGI of 73.5, compared with 71.2 for the open-source closed-loop baseline OpenAPS. Ablations further show that individual adaptation and contextual information improve glucose control. Combining high-frequency rule-based control with lower-frequency agent decisions shows potential for balancing control performance and computational cost. Our work provides a framework, simulator, and evaluation foundation for agents to participate in human physiological control.</p></details> |  |
| **[Four Bridges: Measuring Deception Propensity in a Multi-Agent Game](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03600v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents are increasingly deployed in settings where they interact and coordinate with other agents, yet honesty is mostly measured in single-turn, single-agent benchmarks that pressure the model to deceive. We study deception that emerges in multi-agent interaction without any instruction to deceive. We introduce Kradle, a platform for evaluating AI models, and Four Bridges, an information asymmetry game in which four agents distribute across four rooms: one is the death room, the others hold food worth more if not shared. One agent, the informed agent, knows which room is the death room and can withhold or disclose that information. We evaluate eight frontier models as the informed agent. Deceptive behavior appears in every model and in 73% of all runs (6% to 100% per model), and does not decrease with capability tier within a model family. When the informed agent does disclose, the warning is often challenged, and agents that challenge it enter the death room twice as often as those that do not. Telling all agents that one of them knows the death room collapses deception in six of eight models; telling the informed agent that the room is permanently fatal moves only one. On an open-weight model, an honesty steering vector reduces deception to 0% and adversarial honesty training to 6%. The platform and game provide a testbed for measuring deceptive behavior and comparing interventions.</p></details> | <details><summary>38 pa...</summary><p>38 pages, 14 figures, 16 tables</p></details> |
| **[Spacetime Emergence in the Memory of a Learning Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03596v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We show that the causal structure of spacetime can emerge within the memory of a learning agent performing a quantum information task. We train a model-based reinforcement-learning agent to maximize the quantum mutual information between localized quantum probes interacting with a quantum field in Minkowski spacetime, without directly providing it with information regarding the spacetime metric or causal structure. In learning to accomplish this task, the reinforcement-learning agent constructs an internal predictive model whose planner develops the characteristic null structure of spacetime, allowing us to reconstruct the causal structure of the Minkowski metric with high accuracy. Within this task, spacetime geometry is obtained from an agent's successful predictions and actions, providing a first step toward showing that spacetime concepts emerge from operational quantum experience.</p></details> | 19 pages, 7 figures |
| **[An Agentic AI Workflow for Liver Lesion Segmentation and Structured Radiology Reporting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34599v3)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Despite advances in automated liver lesion segmentation, a gap remains between image-derived predictions and clinically interpretable radiology reports. Bridging this gap requires quantitative measurements that are both reproducible and traceable. We present an evidence-grounded agentic AI framework that connects liver and lesion segmentation with deterministic quantification and structured reporting. TotalSegmentator-based nnU-Net models delineate the liver, Couinaud segments, and lesions, while computational tools extract volumes, dimensions, counts, and anatomical locations. A large language model (LLM) organizes these measurements into a predefined reporting schema, with numerical consistency checks against source measurements. On 51 LiTS cases, the segmentation stage achieved a liver Dice score of 0.947, lesion sensitivity of 75.6%, and positive predictive value of 68.9%. Exploratory screening of 25,494 MERLIN reports identified 10,208 (40.0%) with liver-related abnormalities. By connecting quantitative imaging evidence with agentic report generation, this work takes a step toward human-centered medical AI that prioritizes traceability and clinical relevance.</p></details> |  |
| **[FedReady: Bounded Agents for Federation Formation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03586v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Federated learning (FL) runtimes standardize aggregation and deployment, but each new study still requires federation formation: selecting task-eligible sites and preparing their local data and task code for federated training or analysis. This preparation recurs when the target changes, while raw records and site-private mappings are excluded from cross-site FL messages. We present FedReady, a system that performs federation formation with bounded LLM agents. Agents propose shared contracts, local adapters, and task code; the runtime owns workflow state and accepts artifacts only after checks tied to their digests. Candidate site discovery prioritizes recall; local eligibility verification checks task support before site admission. Once participating sites and artifacts are fixed, conventional federated training or analysis proceeds without readiness agents. Across 39 simulated retinal sites and four tasks, discovery reaches recall 1.00 at precision 0.65; local eligibility verification removes all 17 false positives without losing an eligible site-task pair. The participating sites supply 45,575 task-record instances and complete four 100-round jobs, including one with a runtime-generated contract. Across ten patient cohorts, preparation supports seven patient populations and holds endpoints at three sources as unsupported. Comparison with manually prepared reference populations and analysis (the manual reference) gives a maximum survival-curve difference of 0.001133. We also analyze 19 development failures, targeted classification ablations, and standalone and heterogeneous agent configurations. These results establish the feasibility of task-specific data preparation before ordinary FL training or analysis.</p></details> |  |
| **[TAMPAgent: Agentic Belief-Space Planning for Mobile Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03584v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open-world robotic mobile manipulation requires acting in physical environments with only partial information. While recent Large Vision-Language Model (LVLM) agents show strong open-world reasoning capabilities, existing architectures that rely on direct tool calling struggle with geometric uncertainty and thus are limited to fully-observable tabletop environments. In contrast, Task and Motion Planning (TAMP) systems can solve long-horizon tasks under partial observability but incur high computational costs and require closed worlds. We propose \textbf{TAMPAgent}, an agentic planning framework that unifies the reasoning of LVLMs with the structured constraint satisfaction of TAMP systems. We endow an LVLM agent with a belief-space planning model operating over an extensible API of perception, planning, and control tools. The agent formulates a partial task plan composed of parameterized observation and execution actions, which is subsequently completed using classical search algorithms. After extracting the plan's geometric constraints, the agent leverages its tool API to bind valid parameter values, satisfying feasibility requirements and producing an executable motion plan. Upon execution, the agent continuously updates its belief history and repeats this closed-loop process by interleaving observation and manipulation actions. Evaluated across complex household mobile manipulation tasks on Rainbow Y1 and Unitree G1 platforms, TAMPAgent significantly outperforms direct tool-calling agentic baselines, demonstrating that integrating TAMP constraint satisfaction into agentic planning loops is essential for unstructured, partially observable environments.</p></details> | <details><summary>Websi...</summary><p>Website link: https://umishra.me/TAMPAgent/</p></details> |
| **[VeriHarm-Bench: Measuring Harmful Success in LLM Coding Agents Verifiably](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03575v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) coding agents increasingly operate with shell access, allowing them to write scripts, move files, alter permissions, and modify system configurations. However, an agent may achieve a user's goal while causing unintended harm, such as deleting unrelated files, weakening access controls, or corrupting data, in response to benign inputs without explicit jailbreaking. This failure mode is particularly important because apparent task success may reassure users that an agent behaved correctly, causing harmful side effects to go unnoticed. We call this harmful success: the agent successfully completes the intended benign task while simultaneously causing unintended harm. Despite its importance, harmful success is difficult to measure because conventional task-success metrics overlook harmful behaviors, while existing safety evaluations are often designed solely to assess harmful outcomes. We introduce VeriHarm, an automatic framework for constructing programmatically verifiable benchmarks that directly measure harmful success. VeriHarm minimally perturbs coding tasks to elicit unintended harmful behavior while maintaining the benignity of the task and preserving the original task objective. It then synthesizes executable harm tests that, together with the original task-correctness tests, evaluate both task accuracy and harmful outcomes. Frontier agents, including GPT-5.6-Sol and Claude-Opus-5, achieve an average task accuracy of 95% on our curated VeriHarm-Bench, yet still struggle to complete tasks safely: their safe success rate, the fraction of tasks completed successfully without triggering the designated harm, averages only 37%. These results suggest that successful task completion can frequently coexist with unintended harm, and that task-success metrics alone may substantially understate the potential safety risks of coding agents.</p></details> | Preprint |
| **[MCP Tool Poisoning in Agentic AI Frameworks: A Cross-Framework Measurement Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03555v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The Model Context Protocol (MCP) lets agentic AI frameworks connect language models to external tool providers, but it carries attacker-controlled content (tool descriptions, schema fields, and response data) into model context without validation. Prior benchmarks measure model compliance with poisoned instructions without confirming real execution. We present the first cross-framework measurement of MCP tool poisoning with real tool execution. A 25-class root-cause taxonomy unifies prior classifications, and the open-source tool mcpvenom drives a 9,720-cell susceptibility matrix across 12 frameworks, 18 models, and 45 attack scenarios, with a dual oracle that confirms exfiltration via canary HTTP receipt. The overall attack success rate is 22.9% (2,113 vulnerable cells). Of these, 64.9% dispatch an attacker tool call, 23.0% contact the attacker endpoint, and 12.1% verifiably exfiltrate a secret. Resistance is model-level: nine core frameworks cluster within 23 to 26% (chi-squared = 3.30, p = 0.91), and no framework implements intentional defense. Incidental framework properties mitigate in specific cases, and a regex description scanner rejects 20 of 45 scenarios at a 7.2% false-positive rate, so metadata scanning alone is a partial control. Model ASR ranges from 0.4% to 49.7%. Claude Opus 4.8 is vulnerable in 8 of 530 cells while issuing 223 explicit refusals. A code-level audit finds no intentional defense across twelve frameworks, and all five coordinated disclosures across four vendors were rejected.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the 19th Workshop on Artificial Intelligence and Security (AISec '26), co-located with ACM CCS 2026, The Hague, Netherlands. 12 pages, 3 figures, 9 tables; 4-page supplement included as ancillary file</p></details> |
| **[Can LLM Agents Be Forward-Deployed Engineers? Requirement Discovery and Delivery for Enterprise Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03551v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM coding agents can increasingly automate model post-training, but whether they can act as forward-deployed engineer (FDE) agents, discovering customer requirements and turning them into deployment decisions, remains unclear. We study this on a real enterprise text-to-SQL engagement: a simulated customer, grounded in the real use case's requirements, starts with an ambiguous request. An agent must discover acceptance criteria, data permissions, and hardware constraints in the discovery phase, then plan and execute a post-training recipe in the delivery phase. Across 15 models, the strongest discover over 80% of withheld requirements and open-weight models discover 40-50%; in the delivery phase, only 25% of runs across eight coding-agent configurations pass all acceptance criteria. We identify four bottlenecks in this engagement: (1) incomplete discovery, with 40% of withheld requirements missing or misrepresented; (2) unreliable execution; (3) underused compute budgets and a lack of systematic comparisons across base models and post-training methods; and (4) benchmark accuracy does not guarantee production readiness. We suggest human review of discovered requirements, monitoring of the agent's plan during execution, and an explicit experiment-environment specification covering hardware, budget scope, and production checks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Can LLM Agents Improve Imperfect Earth System Model Parameterizations?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03543v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Earth system models (ESMs) rely on parameterizations to represent unresolved processes such as convection, cloud microphysics, and turbulence. However, translating advances in physical understanding into stable, validated parameterizations and integrating them into ESMs often takes years. Agentic AI could accelerate this process and enable more systematic exploration of design choices. Using warm-rain collision-coalescence as a test case, we evaluate agents' ability to synthesize scientific literature, reimplement published bulk parameterizations, and propose new functional forms as differentiable JAX code. We compare conventional parameterizations, data-driven emulators, and agent-generated closures against high-fidelity superdroplet simulations, repeating unattended experiments across ten language models. With expert guidance or iterative feedback, agents produced executable, differentiable, mass-conserving code, although unattended performance depended strongly on the scheme and language model. | **[Toward Knowledge-Centric AI: Discovering, Internalizing, and Using Explicit Knowledge Across Agents and Generations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03157v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Knowledge is how understanding accumulates across people and generations: durable records that outlive their producers and that others can check, combine, teach, and correct. We train three workflows (Eval-Opt, Voting, Orch-Workers) with GRPO on math and code at three Qwen3 scales, with one policy for all roles (Shared-Policy, SP) or one per role type (Isolated-Policy, IP). Every workflow gains by adapting to its structure; this does not make a role better than training it alone. Single-agent RL in the same structure matches or beats trained Voting and Orch-Workers, and within the grid only Eval-Opt under IP exceeds it. Eval-Opt's evaluator learns when to retry and its generator learns to complete the retried attempts, a joint strategy that a single policy trained alone does not acquire. Under SP, per-output token averaging sets each role's gradient share, a hidden hyperparameter absent from single-agent RL, and the solving roles learn slowly; reweighting it gives SP the speed and peak of IP, and its decline. Under IP, a parallel role, whose instances share one adapter, takes one optimizer step per instance per training step and can drift after the peak as its entropy rises and accuracy declines. Merging those steps delays the decline. Our code is at: https://github.com/XHMY/marl-llm-workflows</p></details> |  |
